- How to Decide — mapped book reference page (Folder_ID 0444)
- Box source: Chapter_07.txt (file ID 2594905365591; Chapter 7 content ends before the next chapter begins)
58. Q: What should be documented when a team uses these decision tools for an important organizational choice?
A: Show answer
A useful record should identify the objective, acceptable criteria, main options, plausible outcomes, material risks, relevant probabilities or assumptions, the cost of changing course, and any information that could alter the preferred option. It should also explain why further information was or was not obtained and why the decision was considered good enough. For a team, recording the reasoning helps distinguish a deliberate time-saving choice from an unsupported shortcut. It also creates a basis for learning when new evidence or outcomes become available.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page decision note for a real team choice, including the selected tool, assumptions, unresolved uncertainties, and review trigger.
Key point:
- A concise decision record makes assumptions and stopping logic visible.
- Documentation supports later learning without requiring every decision to become a lengthy report.

59. Q: How can a person review a decision after the outcome without confusing outcome quality with decision quality?
A: Show answer
The chapter's framework encourages prospective thinking and evaluation of possibilities, payoffs, and probabilities rather than judging the process solely by what happened. A favorable result does not prove that the choice was well reasoned, and an unfavorable result does not automatically prove that the decision was poor. A review should ask what information was available at the time, whether the options were sorted appropriately, whether the time spent was proportionate to the impact, and what the experience reveals about preferences or assumptions. This turns outcomes into learning rather than hindsight-based blame.
Study instruction:
- After an important result, reconstruct what was knowable at the time and identify one process improvement separately from the outcome itself.
Key point:
- Judge the reasoning using information available when the decision was made.
- Use experience to improve future choices rather than relying only on hindsight.

- How to Decide — mapped book reference page (Folder_ID 0444)
- Box source: Chapter_07.txt (file ID 2594905365591; Chapter 7 content ends before the next chapter begins)
60. Q: How can the framework be turned into a repeatable personal decision protocol?
A: Show answer
A practical protocol begins by defining the goal and essential criteria, then estimating the impact of an imperfect outcome. Apply the Happiness Test for low-impact choices, check whether the option repeats, and look for favorable upside-downside asymmetry. If several close options pass the Only-Option Test, stop trying to extract tiny differences. Assess the cost of quitting and consider decision stacking or parallel trials where useful. Finally, ask whether obtainable information could change the choice and whether its cost is justified. Decide when no worthwhile information remains.
Study instruction:
- Write a short personal checklist using these steps and test it on one routine choice and one consequential choice.
Key point:
- Use a consistent sequence to match decision effort to impact and reversibility.
- A protocol should support judgment, not replace it when material risks or duties require more care.
Source links:
- How to Decide — mapped book reference page (Folder_ID 0444)
- Box source: Chapter_07.txt (file ID 2594905365591; Chapter 7 content ends before the next chapter begins)
61. Q: What is the chapter's overall principle for deciding when to go fast and when to slow down?
A: Show answer
The chapter's overall principle is to allocate decision time according to the expected penalty of being wrong and the value of additional information. Go faster when the decision is low impact, repeats, offers limited downside, involves several similarly good options, can be reversed cheaply, or can be tested in parallel. Slow down when the downside is substantial, opportunity cost is high, the choice is difficult to reverse, or decision-relevant information is available at a reasonable cost. The goal is a good-enough decision supported by proportionate analysis, not certainty at any price.
Study instruction:
- Summarize a current decision in one paragraph using impact, downside, repeatability, closeness of options, reversibility, and information cost.
Key point:
- Decision speed should be proportional to consequence and information value.
- Good judgment combines deliberate sorting with timely selection and a willingness to learn.

Table 8
The Power of Negative Thinking and Decision Execution
1. Q: What is the central argument of the chapter The Power of Negative Thinking?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that setting an attractive goal and imagining success are not enough to produce reliable execution. People often know what they intend to do but later make choices that undermine it. Deliberately examining how a goal or decision could fail helps expose obstacles, weak assumptions, and self-defeating behavior while there is still time to respond. This is not a recommendation to become pessimistic or abandon ambition. It is a disciplined way to combine a positive destination with a realistic assessment of the route, including controllable actions, external events, and the reactions that may follow either success or failure. The chapter develops this approach through premortems, backcasting, precommitments, the Dr. Evil game, preparation for tilt, and hedging.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a realistic review of failure paths can strengthen a positive goal without replacing it with pessimism."
Key point:
- Positive goals need an execution plan that anticipates obstacles.
- Negative thinking is useful when it produces practical changes to a decision or plan.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
2. Q: What is the behavior gap, and why does it matter to decision-making?
A: Show answer
The behavior gap is the difference between what a person knows or intends to do and the decisions the person actually makes later. Someone may intend to exercise regularly, meet a deadline, save money, or follow a sound hiring process, yet repeatedly choose actions that conflict with that intention. The gap is not necessarily caused by ignorance. It can arise from immediate temptations, friction, emotional reactions, social pressure, poor planning, or a failure to recognize cumulative effects. The chapter treats execution as a central decision problem: a goal is only useful if future choices and circumstances allow it to be pursued. Tools such as precommitments and category decisions reduce the number of vulnerable in-the-moment choices.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one goal and list the specific future decisions that could create a gap between intention and action."
Key point:
- Knowing what should be done does not ensure that it will be done.
- Designing the decision environment can help close the behavior gap.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Klusmann et al.: Mental Contrasting and Behavior Change
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
3. Q: Why can people identify outdated past beliefs more easily than beliefs they hold today?
A: Show answer
The opening exercise asks readers to recall beliefs they once defended strongly but now regard as weak, and then to identify current beliefs they may later reconsider. Many people can readily list past beliefs because they can see them from a distance and know how events unfolded. Current beliefs feel more convincing because people are still immersed in the information, identity, relationships, and assumptions that support them. This contrast exposes a tendency to treat present confidence as stronger evidence than it really is. The exercise is not proof that current beliefs are wrong; it is a prompt to recognize that today's certainty may later look incomplete. A useful response is to identify assumptions that could be tested and evidence that would justify changing one's mind.
Study instruction:
- "Write down one strongly held current belief and specify what future evidence would make you revise it."
Key point:
- Confidence in a present belief does not guarantee that it will withstand later evidence.
- Explicitly naming assumptions makes future revision easier.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
4. Q: What is mental contrasting?
A: Show answer
Mental contrasting means considering a desired future while also confronting the obstacles that stand between the present situation and that future. Rather than imagining only the reward, a person identifies the practical barriers that could prevent the reward from occurring. For example, someone who wants to finish a qualification might picture the benefit of completing it and then examine competing work demands, weak study routines, travel, or lack of access to materials. The contrast turns a vague wish into a more realistic appraisal of what achieving it requires. In the chapter, mental contrasting is the foundation for negative thinking: the aim is to discover actionable obstacles, not to rehearse failure for its own sake. Research on mental contrasting also examines how it can be combined with concrete if-then plans.
Study instruction:
- "For one goal, write the desired outcome and then identify the main internal and external obstacles that could block it."
Key point:
- A goal becomes more actionable when its obstacles are made explicit.
- Mental contrasting links aspiration with realistic preparation.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Klusmann et al.: Mental Contrasting and Behavior Change
5. Q: What is prospective hindsight, and how does it support a premortem?
A: Show answer
Prospective hindsight is the practice of imagining that a future event has already happened and then looking backward to explain how it occurred. Instead of asking only what might go wrong, the decision-maker adopts the perspective that the goal has failed and asks why. This framing encourages concrete causal explanations rather than generic statements about risk. It can make it easier to identify overlooked events, choices, and dependencies. The underlying research examines how people explain an event when it is framed as certain rather than merely possible; it does not mean that imagining an outcome makes it inevitable. In the chapter, prospective hindsight is combined with mental contrasting to create the premortem.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a vague risk question as a future-failure explanation beginning with the assumption that the goal has already failed."
Key point:
- Prospective hindsight changes the perspective from prediction to explanation.
- An imagined outcome is a reasoning device, not evidence that the outcome will occur.
Source links:
- Mitchell, Russo and Pennington: Prospective Hindsight
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
6. Q: What is a premortem, and how is it different from a postmortem?
A: Show answer
A premortem is an exercise conducted before a decision or project has played out. Participants imagine that the goal has failed at a specified future date and work backward to identify plausible reasons. A postmortem examines an outcome after it has occurred. A postmortem can generate valuable lessons, but it cannot prevent the already completed failure and may be affected by hindsight or outcome-based judgments. A premortem moves the learning opportunity earlier, when the plan can still be changed. It does not replace ordinary risk analysis or guarantee that all risks will be found. Its value is that it gives people permission to identify weaknesses and dissenting views before commitment makes revision more difficult.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a premortem and a postmortem for the same project and identify which decisions can still be changed at each stage."
Key point:
- A premortem is preventive; a postmortem is retrospective.
- The premortem is most useful while the plan remains adjustable.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
7. Q: What are the main steps for conducting an individual premortem?
A: Show answer
First, identify a specific goal or decision rather than a vague aspiration. Second, choose a reasonable time horizon in which the goal should be achieved or the decision should have produced an observable result. Third, imagine that the goal was not achieved or the decision worked badly, and list plausible reasons arising from your own decisions and execution. Fourth, separately list reasons arising from events or people outside your control. The chapter's exercise encourages several distinct reasons in each category rather than a single explanation. Finally, examine whether the exercise revealed obstacles that were not previously considered and decide what should change. The result should be a practical risk picture that informs action, not merely a list kept for reference.
Study instruction:
- "Run the five-step exercise for a real goal and preserve separate lists for controllable and external causes."
Key point:
- A clear goal and time horizon make the imagined failure concrete.
- The exercise is complete only when its findings are considered in the plan.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
8. Q: Why should premortem causes be separated into factors within and outside one's control?
A: Show answer
The distinction prevents two opposite errors. A person may blame bad luck for a failure that was partly caused by poor preparation, weak execution, or an avoidable decision. Conversely, a person may assume complete personal control and ignore external conditions such as regulatory changes, market shifts, weather, supplier failure, or another party's actions. The chapter therefore asks participants to identify causes arising from their own decisions or their team's actions separately from causes outside their influence. This separation improves the response: controllable causes may require different actions, responsibilities, or safeguards, while external causes may require contingency plans, monitoring, or hedges. The categories are not always perfectly clear, so shared or partially controllable causes should be described rather than forced into a false binary.
Study instruction:
- "Classify each risk in a current plan as controllable, external, or shared, and explain the reason for the classification."
Key point:
- Responsibility and exposure to external uncertainty require different responses.
- Do not confuse influence with total control.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
9. Q: How can a premortem be applied to a personal goal such as exercise or completing an assignment?
A: Show answer
The person first defines the target and a realistic period, such as exercising on planned days for six months or submitting an assignment by its deadline. The person then imagines the period has ended and the goal was missed. Reasons might include relying on motivation rather than scheduling, underestimating travel time, accepting too many competing commitments, failing to prepare equipment, or not seeking help when the work becomes difficult. External causes might include illness, a family emergency, a service disruption, or an unexpected change in work responsibilities. The person should then decide which causes can be prevented, detected early, or mitigated. The exercise is more useful when it produces changes such as a protected calendar slot, an earlier start date, or a backup arrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the exercise to one personal goal and convert the three most actionable failure causes into specific preventive actions."
Key point:
- Personal goals often fail through ordinary friction and competing commitments.
- Practical safeguards are more useful than relying on stronger willpower alone.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
10. Q: How can a premortem improve a business or hiring decision?
A: Show answer
For a business decision, the team can imagine that the venture, launch, or investment has failed after a defined period and work backward to identify why. Possible causes may include weak customer demand, an unrealistic valuation, inability to raise capital, poor cash management, loss of key staff, an unsuitable business model, or a competitor entering the market. For a hiring decision, the team can imagine that the candidate has left within a year and identify causes such as unclear role expectations, poor management, inadequate resources, misaligned incentives, or an inaccurate assessment of the person's skills. The purpose is not to assume that failure is likely. It is to test whether the current plan has blind spots and to improve the decision before commitments become costly.
Study instruction:
- "Run a premortem for a pending hire or business initiative and distinguish selection risks from onboarding and execution risks."
Key point:
- A premortem can expose risks in both the initial choice and its implementation.
- The findings should change due diligence, expectations, or contingency planning where warranted.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
11. Q: Why are premortems useful for teams, and how can they reduce groupthink?
A: Show answer
Teams can contain more information and perspectives than any one member, but ordinary discussion may not reveal that diversity. Members may follow an emerging consensus, defer to senior colleagues, avoid appearing negative, or assume that someone else will raise a concern. The chapter explains that this can leave a group with many people expressing the same inside view. A premortem changes the social task: identifying ways the plan failed becomes the assignment, so dissent is useful rather than disloyal. It can surface overlooked operational knowledge, hidden dependencies, and weaknesses that participants were reluctant to mention during a conventional planning meeting. The facilitator must still ensure that concerns are examined rather than accepted automatically.
Study instruction:
- "Identify where status or consensus pressure could suppress dissent in a team decision and design a premortem to counter it."
Key point:
- More participants do not automatically produce more independent thinking.
- A structured invitation to challenge the plan can improve the information available to a team.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
12. Q: Why should team members complete a premortem independently before discussing their findings?
A: Show answer
Independent preparation reduces the chance that early speakers or senior participants will shape everyone else's list. If a team starts with open discussion, members may anchor on the first explanations, conform to a dominant view, or withhold a concern that seems unpopular. Having each person write possible causes privately before the group discussion preserves a wider range of initial judgments. The group can then share and combine reasons, identify duplicates, clarify evidence, and assess which risks require action. This sequence does not eliminate hierarchy or groupthink, but it makes it harder for consensus to form before different perspectives have been recorded. It also gives quieter members a defined opportunity to contribute.
Study instruction:
- "Facilitate a premortem using silent individual writing first, followed by a round in which every participant contributes."
Key point:
- Independent generation protects diversity before group discussion.
- A facilitator should prevent the first or loudest voice from defining the risk list.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
13. Q: What are the limitations of a premortem?
A: Show answer
A premortem does not predict the future, calculate the true probability of every risk, or guarantee that the team will recognize all important failure paths. Participants may overlook unfamiliar events, exaggerate vivid threats, focus on causes that are easy to imagine, or produce a list that is never translated into action. It can also become an exercise in blame if participants use it to criticize individuals rather than examine decisions and systems. The imagined time horizon may be too short or too long, and causes may be incorrectly classified as controllable or external. A sound premortem therefore needs evidence, diverse perspectives, proportionate risk assessment, assigned responses, and later review. It should complement, not replace, technical analysis and formal risk management.
Study instruction:
- "Review a premortem for speculative risks, missing evidence, unclear ownership, and actions that were never assigned."
Key point:
- A premortem is a structured imagination exercise, not a substitute for evidence.
- Its value depends on the quality of the follow-through.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
14. Q: What is backcasting, and how does it differ from a premortem?
A: Show answer
Backcasting starts by imagining that the goal has been achieved or the decision has worked well, then looks backward to identify the reasons for success. It is the positive counterpart to the premortem. The person specifies the goal and time period, imagines the successful future, and lists the decisions, actions, team contributions, and external conditions that helped produce the outcome. A premortem examines plausible failure paths; a backcast examines plausible success paths. Backcasting is not simply daydreaming about a reward because the exercise asks how success was achieved. It helps identify enabling actions and favorable conditions that can be deliberately encouraged or preserved.
Study instruction:
- "Use the same goal and time horizon for a backcast that you used for the premortem, then list the actions that made success possible."
Key point:
- Backcasting works backward from a defined successful outcome.
- It identifies enabling paths, not just an attractive future image.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
15. Q: Why should a decision-maker combine a premortem with backcasting?
A: Show answer
Using only a premortem can produce an overly negative picture, while using only a backcast can reinforce optimism and understate obstacles. The chapter recommends combining the two to obtain a more balanced view of the future. The premortem challenges overconfidence and the illusion that the plan will naturally work. The backcast helps correct excessive pessimism by identifying plausible ways to succeed. Together they reveal both routes to failure and routes to success, including actions that can improve the odds and external conditions that may help or hinder. The aim is not to average pessimism and optimism mechanically, but to understand both sets of causal pathways before deciding how to proceed.
Study instruction:
- "Place the failure reasons and success reasons side by side and identify actions that reduce the former while strengthening the latter."
Key point:
- A balanced future view includes both failure paths and success paths.
- Premortems and backcasts are complementary rather than competing exercises.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
16. Q: What is a Decision Exploration Table, and what information should it contain?
A: Show answer
A Decision Exploration Table brings the results of a premortem and a backcast into one view. It records reasons that could contribute to failure and reasons that could contribute to success, including factors within and outside the decision-maker's control. The chapter adds an estimate of the likelihood of each item so that the decision-maker can distinguish plausible, high-priority events from remote possibilities. A useful working table can also record the potential impact, evidence or indicators, an owner, and the response to be taken. Likelihood estimates should be treated as judgments rather than precise measurements unless supporting data exist. The table is a planning aid: it makes assumptions visible and helps prioritize attention.
Study instruction:
- "Create a table with event, success or failure direction, controllability, likelihood, impact, evidence, and response."
Key point:
- The table combines the two future perspectives in one decision aid.
- Likelihood and impact help prioritize which events deserve attention.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
17. Q: How should likelihood estimates in a Decision Exploration Table be used?
A: Show answer
Likelihood estimates help compare the reasons identified in the premortem and backcast because not all events are equally likely. A low-probability event with catastrophic impact may deserve attention, while a frequent but minor inconvenience may require only a routine response. Estimates should be calibrated to the available evidence and expressed with an appropriate level of precision. Where exact probabilities are not defensible, qualitative ranges or broad bands may be more honest than invented numerical accuracy. The estimates should also be revisited when new information appears. They are not guarantees and should not be treated as objective facts merely because they appear in a table. Their purpose is to improve prioritization and expose assumptions for discussion.
Study instruction:
- "For each major event, record the basis for its likelihood estimate and identify what new evidence would change the estimate."
Key point:
- Likelihood estimates are decision aids, not promises about what will happen.
- Combine probability with impact and update estimates as evidence changes.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
18. Q: What should a decision-maker do after completing the premortem, backcast, and Decision Exploration Table?
A: Show answer
The first question is whether the original goal or decision should be modified or abandoned in light of what has been learned. If a major obstacle makes the plan unattractive or exposes an unacceptable risk, changing course may be wiser than trying to force the original plan to work. For example, a company considering entry into a new market might delay the launch if a likely regulatory change could prohibit the product. If there are still good reasons to proceed, the analysis can be used to modify the decision, increase the likelihood of favorable conditions, prepare responses to foreseeable events, and mitigate the impact of downside events. The key is to make the analysis consequential rather than treating it as paperwork.
Study instruction:
- "Write a decision statement after the table: proceed, modify, delay, or stop, and explain which findings drove the choice."
Key point:
- The first use of risk analysis may be to change the decision itself.
- If proceeding, convert identified risks and opportunities into concrete adjustments.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
19. Q: What is a precommitment contract?
A: Show answer
A precommitment contract is an agreement made in advance to take or avoid certain actions, or to change the barriers surrounding those actions. It can be an agreement with another person or a commitment made to oneself. The purpose is to influence a future choice before the person is exposed to the temptation, stress, or short-term incentive that may undermine the current intention. A precommitment can make an undesirable action physically unavailable, increase the effort required to take it, or make a desirable action easier to perform. It does not guarantee perfect behavior. It changes the decision environment so that a future self has fewer opportunities to abandon a considered plan impulsively.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one predictable future temptation and design a commitment made before that temptation occurs."
Key point:
- A precommitment moves part of the decision from the pressured moment to an earlier, calmer moment.
- It changes future options or friction rather than relying only on willpower.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
20. Q: What is a Ulysses contract, and why is it used as an example of precommitment?
A: Show answer
A Ulysses contract is a commitment made in advance to constrain a future action that the person expects may become tempting. In the story used by the chapter, Ulysses knows that the Sirens' song could cause him to steer the ship toward danger. He therefore arranges in advance for the crew to block their hearing and for himself to be tied to the mast. The important feature is that he recognizes a predictable conflict between his present judgment and his future impulse, then designs a constraint before the impulse arrives. Modern precommitments are usually less dramatic, but the logic is similar: reduce the ability to make a decision that has already been identified as harmful.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a situation in which your future preference may differ from your present considered intention, then design a proportionate safeguard."
Key point:
- A Ulysses contract anticipates a future conflict of interests within the same decision-maker.
- The constraint is arranged before the tempting situation occurs.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
21. Q: What three broad forms of precommitment does the chapter describe?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three broad forms. First, a person may physically prevent an unwanted action, as Ulysses was prevented from steering the ship. Second, a person may raise barriers so that an undesirable action becomes more difficult or costly, creating friction and a moment to reconsider. Third, a person may lower barriers so that a desirable action becomes easier to perform. These forms can be used separately or together. For example, removing tempting food raises the barrier to an unwanted choice, while preparing healthy food lowers the barrier to a preferred choice. The design should target the actual obstacle and remain proportionate to the stakes.
Study instruction:
- "For one goal, identify a physical constraint, a barrier to raise, and a barrier to lower."
Key point:
- Precommitment can restrict, discourage, or facilitate future actions.
- The most useful design addresses the specific behavior that threatens the goal.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
22. Q: How can physical constraints help prevent decisions that conflict with a goal?
A: Show answer
A physical constraint removes or restricts access to an action that the person has already identified as harmful. The chapter's examples include arranging transport so that someone does not drive after drinking and using an arrangement that prevents an impulsive action. A physical constraint can be powerful because it does not require the person to win a fresh argument with temptation at the critical moment. However, it should be safe, lawful, proportionate, and reversible when circumstances legitimately change. It should not create a risk greater than the behavior it is intended to prevent. Where another person is involved, consent and clear responsibility are important; precommitment should not become coercion or an excuse to remove legitimate autonomy.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate whether a proposed physical constraint is effective, proportionate, safe, and consistent with the person's rights."
Key point:
- Physical constraints can remove a high-risk option before temptation arises.
- A safeguard must be proportionate and must not create new harms.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
23. Q: What does it mean to raise a barrier to an undesirable action?
A: Show answer
Raising a barrier means increasing the effort, delay, inconvenience, or accountability associated with an action that could undermine a goal. The chapter gives examples such as placing an alarm clock away from the bed or removing tempting food from the home. These measures do not make the action impossible, but they create friction and a pause in which the person can reconsider. The barrier is most useful when the unwanted behavior is easy to repeat and each individual instance seems harmless. A well-designed barrier should be specific enough to interrupt the behavior without making ordinary life unnecessarily difficult. It can also be reviewed if the underlying goal or circumstances change.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a repeated unwanted action and add one small, measurable source of friction before it can occur."
Key point:
- Friction can interrupt automatic or impulsive behavior.
- A barrier should target the behavior without creating unnecessary inconvenience elsewhere.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
24. Q: What does it mean to lower a barrier to a desirable action?
A: Show answer
Lowering a barrier means reducing the time, effort, uncertainty, or preparation required to perform an action that supports the goal. The chapter's examples include stocking a home with healthy food, packing a lunch, or automating transfers into savings. These arrangements make the preferred behavior more convenient at the moment of choice. Lowering barriers is the positive counterpart to making undesirable behavior harder. It is particularly useful when the person already wants to act but repeatedly fails because the desired behavior requires too many steps or too much preparation. A lower barrier should not remove necessary safeguards, such as review or approval in a financial process.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one desirable behavior and remove a practical obstacle by preparing the materials, timing, or process in advance."
Key point:
- Convenience can support consistency by making the preferred action easier to start.
- Simplification should not bypass essential controls or judgment.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
25. Q: How do implementation intentions and if-then plans support precommitment?
A: Show answer
An implementation intention specifies in advance what a person will do when a particular situation occurs. It is commonly expressed as an if-then plan: if a defined obstacle or trigger appears, then a defined response will follow. For example, if an evening meeting prevents a planned workout, then the person will use a shorter morning session the next day. The plan links a recognizable cue to a prepared action, reducing the need to improvise under pressure. Research reviews describe implementation intentions as a self-regulation strategy that can help translate decisions into action. Such plans work best when the cue is observable and the response is feasible; a vague promise to try harder is not an implementation intention.
Study instruction:
- "Write two if-then plans for the most likely obstacles identified in your premortem and test whether each cue is observable."
Key point:
- An if-then plan connects a specific trigger with a prepared response.
- The plan must be realistic enough to carry out when the trigger occurs.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
- Klusmann et al.: Mental Contrasting and Behavior Change
26. Q: What is the Dr. Evil game, and what problem is it designed to reveal?
A: Show answer
The Dr. Evil game asks a person to imagine an agent deliberately steering them toward failure through decisions that look reasonable when considered one at a time. The imagined agent cannot make obviously reckless choices because those would be noticed. Instead, it relies on repeated small exceptions that each have a plausible justification but accumulate into a pattern that defeats the goal. The game exposes behavior that is difficult to recognize at the level of a single decision. It shifts attention from dramatic mistakes to recurring categories of apparently harmless choices. The chapter adapts the exercise from Dan Egan and uses it to identify where a person's future self may subtly sabotage a present intention.
Study instruction:
- "For one goal, list three individually defensible choices that would cause failure if repeated over time."
Key point:
- Repeated small exceptions can undermine a goal without any single choice looking decisive.
- The game reveals patterns that are easy to miss when decisions are viewed separately.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
27. Q: What are the steps for playing the Dr. Evil game?
A: Show answer
Begin by naming a positive goal. Next, imagine that Dr. Evil controls your decisions and wants you to fail without being detected. Generate choices that would move you away from the goal while remaining easy to justify in each individual instance. Record the choices and examine what they have in common. The important output is not a list of outrageous acts; it is a set of recurring decision types that can pass as reasonable exceptions. Once those categories are visible, decide how to make them less automatic, how to monitor their frequency, and whether a precommitment or category decision would reduce exposure. The exercise should end with a protective decision rule, not simply a catalogue of weaknesses.
Study instruction:
- "Complete the four-step game and identify the repeated decision category that poses the greatest cumulative risk."
Key point:
- The Dr. Evil game focuses on plausible, repeated choices rather than obvious disasters.
- The next step is to design a safeguard for the identified category.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
28. Q: Why can individually reasonable decisions create a pattern that guarantees failure?
A: Show answer
A decision may appear harmless when evaluated in isolation but become damaging when repeated. A single skipped workout, discretionary purchase, late start, or exception to a process may have little effect. Repeated often enough, the same choices can consume the time, money, or consistency needed to reach the goal. The chapter compares this with a series of small cuts: the cumulative effect is much larger than any one event suggests. The risk is increased when people treat every instance as unique and fail to track frequency. A useful response is to evaluate decisions at both the individual and category level, looking at their aggregate impact over a week, month, or longer period.
Study instruction:
- "Review recent exceptions as a group and calculate their cumulative effect on time, money, or progress."
Key point:
- A series of small losses can be more damaging than one obvious mistake.
- Aggregate patterns matter when evaluating repeated decisions.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
29. Q: What is a category decision, and how does it protect a goal?
A: Show answer
A category decision is a choice made in advance about which options are available or unavailable whenever a particular type of decision arises. Instead of reconsidering the same vulnerable choice every time, the person establishes a rule for the whole category. The chapter contrasts a firm identity-based rule, such as not considering certain foods, with a softer intention to consume less of them. The softer intention leaves the person facing the same tempting decision repeatedly. A category decision reduces the number of in-the-moment opportunities for rationalization. It should be chosen carefully, because a rigid rule may be inappropriate where circumstances vary or exceptions are genuinely necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a recurring decision category and write a clear advance rule, including any legitimate exception criteria."
Key point:
- A category decision replaces repeated case-by-case choices with an advance rule.
- The rule should be clear while allowing justified exceptions where necessary.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
30. Q: How can category decisions help investors stay within a circle of competence?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes professional investors who limit the types of investments they will consider to areas they understand. An attractive opportunity outside that boundary may create pressure to overestimate one's ability, especially when promised returns appear unusually high. A category decision made in advance narrows the set of opportunities that reach detailed consideration. This does not prove that every investment within the category is good or that every outside investment is bad. It is a discipline for avoiding decisions in which the person lacks sufficient knowledge to evaluate the risks. The boundary should be based on a defensible assessment of expertise and should be reviewed as competence develops.
Study instruction:
- "Define the areas in which you have enough knowledge to evaluate an opportunity and the evidence required before expanding that boundary."
Key point:
- A circle-of-competence rule limits exposure to poorly understood choices.
- A category rule reduces the temptation to make exceptions for unusually attractive offers.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
31. Q: What is tilt, and why can it impair decision-making after a bad outcome?
A: Show answer
Tilt is an emotionally hot state following a bad outcome in which the person's decision-making becomes compromised. The chapter explains that a setback can activate emotional reactions that interfere with deliberate reasoning. A person may become angry, panicked, ashamed, or preoccupied with reversing the loss, then make further choices that worsen the situation. For example, an investor might react to market volatility by repeatedly changing a portfolio without a considered strategy. Tilt is not limited to investing; it can occur after a failed exam, a project setback, a rejection, or a mistake in a personal goal. The key is to recognize the state before making another consequential decision.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the emotional and behavioral signs that appear after a setback and specify which decisions should be paused when those signs occur."
Key point:
- A bad outcome can trigger a state in which the next decision is also at risk.
- Recognizing tilt early creates an opportunity to pause and regain perspective.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
32. Q: What is the what-the-hell effect, and how can it derail a goal?
A: Show answer
The what-the-hell effect occurs when a person treats one lapse as proof that the whole effort is already ruined. After eating a snack that conflicts with a diet, for example, the person may conclude that the day is lost and continue making choices that conflict with the goal. The same pattern can appear in studying, budgeting, exercise, or project execution: one missed action becomes a reason to abandon the remaining plan. The chapter's solution is to prepare in advance for imperfection. A precommitment can specify that one lapse will not justify postponing the goal or abandoning the next planned action. The recovery response should be simple and immediate.
Study instruction:
- "Write a recovery rule for one likely lapse, specifying the next constructive action rather than waiting for a new start date."
Key point:
- One poor choice does not require a second poor choice.
- A prepared recovery response prevents a lapse from becoming abandonment.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
33. Q: What is the sunk cost fallacy, and how can it affect a decision after a setback?
A: Show answer
The sunk cost fallacy occurs when a person continues investing in a course of action because of resources already spent, even when those past costs cannot be recovered and the forward-looking case for continuing is weak. The chapter uses a project example: someone may refuse to stop because substantial time or money has already been invested. The emotional difficulty of admitting that the earlier choice was poor can make it harder to evaluate the remaining options objectively. Research on escalation of commitment examines how people can become locked into a costly course of action after setbacks. The practical response is to assess future benefits, future costs, and realistic alternatives rather than treating past expenditure as a reason to continue.
Study instruction:
- "For a struggling project, separate unrecoverable past costs from the additional costs and benefits of continuing today."
Key point:
- Past expenditure is not itself a reason to commit more resources.
- Evaluate the remaining decision on its forward-looking merits.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Staw: The Escalation of Commitment
34. Q: How can a decision-maker distinguish rational persistence from the sunk cost fallacy?
A: Show answer
Rational persistence is based on the expected future value of continuing compared with stopping, changing course, or choosing an alternative. The sunk cost fallacy occurs when past investment exerts influence even though it cannot be recovered. A project may still deserve further resources if new evidence shows that the expected benefits exceed the remaining costs and risks. Conversely, a project should not continue merely because it has consumed a large budget, because stopping would feel like admitting failure, or because the team has publicly defended it. The decision should use current forecasts, realistic alternatives, and pre-agreed criteria. An independent reviewer can help challenge motivated reasoning when the original decision-makers are heavily invested.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a continue, modify, or stop analysis that excludes sunk costs from the forward-looking comparison."
Key point:
- Persistence is justified by future value, not by the size of past investment.
- Independent review can reduce self-justification in high-commitment decisions.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Staw: The Escalation of Commitment
35. Q: What is a tilt inventory, and how should it be used?
A: Show answer
A tilt inventory is a personal list of signals that indicate when emotional reactions are beginning to impair judgment. The chapter suggests looking for physical signs such as a flushed face, cognitive signs such as difficulty organizing thoughts, and behavioral or verbal signs such as taking events personally, becoming confrontational, or repeating negative self-talk. The signs differ among individuals, so the inventory should be based on observed past reactions rather than a generic checklist alone. When several signs appear, the person can use a preplanned pause, seek an outside view, or postpone a non-urgent decision. The inventory is useful only if it leads to an action that reduces the risk of impulsive choices.
Study instruction:
- "Record the physical, cognitive, and behavioral signals that have preceded your poor decisions after setbacks."
Key point:
- Tilt has recognizable personal warning signs.
- A tilt inventory should trigger a specific pause or recovery action.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
36. Q: How can mental time travel and the Happiness Test help someone recover from tilt?
A: Show answer
Mental time travel means considering the present situation from the perspective of a future self. When a person recognizes signs of tilt, the chapter recommends asking whether a decision made right now will still seem acceptable in a week, a month, or a year. The Happiness Test similarly asks the person to consider whether the immediate reaction is likely to be something they will be glad to have done later. This shift can create distance from the emotional intensity of the moment and make longer-term goals more salient. It is not a guarantee that the future self will know the correct answer. It is a structured pause that can prevent an immediate feeling from dictating an irreversible action.
Study instruction:
- "Write a future-self question that you will use before making a high-stakes decision while emotionally activated."
Key point:
- A longer time horizon can interrupt short-term emotional urgency.
- The future-self perspective is a pause for judgment, not a prediction.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
37. Q: How should people precommit to their response when a setback occurs?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends deciding in advance what to do when a foreseeable bad outcome occurs. This may include pausing before a trade or major purchase, asking another person to execute a decision, using a pre-agreed review process, or defining conditions under which a project should be stopped. A person who fears the sunk cost fallacy can write down exit criteria before investing further resources. Someone who fears the what-the-hell effect can commit to resume the next planned action after a lapse rather than abandoning the whole goal. These plans are most effective when the trigger and response are specific, the responsible person is clear, and the plan is feasible under pressure.
Study instruction:
- "For one likely setback, define the trigger, immediate response, decision authority, and criteria for resuming or changing course."
Key point:
- Preplanned responses reduce the need to improvise while emotionally compromised.
- Exit criteria are more credible when set before the setback occurs.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
38. Q: Why can unexpectedly positive outcomes also damage future decision-making?
A: Show answer
A favorable result can create overconfidence just as a bad result can create tilt. The chapter describes people who succeed through an emergency or improvised choice and then conclude that the shortcut is a reliable method. A student who receives a high grade after last-minute study may assume the same approach will work again. A business that hires quickly and happens to find an excellent employee may abandon a more careful selection process. An investor who earns a strong return may overestimate skill and reduce diversification. The lesson is to avoid treating one outcome as conclusive evidence about the quality of the decision process. Success should be reviewed for the role of skill, preparation, luck, and conditions that may not recur.
Study instruction:
- "After an unexpectedly good result, identify which parts came from skill, preparation, and luck before changing your process."
Key point:
- Good outcomes can reinforce poor processes when luck is mistaken for skill.
- Review positive surprises with the same discipline used for failures.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
39. Q: What is hedging in the chapter, and what are its three key features?
A: Show answer
The chapter defines hedging as paying for something that one hopes never to use in order to reduce the impact of a downside event. It identifies three features: a hedge reduces the impact of bad luck when it occurs, it has a cost, and the decision-maker hopes it will not be needed. Insurance is a familiar example because a premium is paid in exchange for protection against a covered loss. Other examples include renting a tent as a weather backup for an outdoor event or leaving extra time before a flight. A hedge does not remove the underlying uncertainty; it changes the consequences if the unfavorable event happens.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a downside event and describe the protective measure, its cost, and the loss it is intended to reduce."
Key point:
- A hedge reduces exposure to a downside event but has an upfront cost.
- Not using a hedge does not automatically mean the original decision was irrational.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
40. Q: How should a decision-maker evaluate whether a hedge is worth its cost?
A: Show answer
The decision-maker should compare the cost of the hedge with the reduction in expected or worst-case loss that it provides, while considering the severity of the event and the decision-maker's ability to absorb the loss. The chapter's examples show that a backup can be sensible when its cost is modest relative to the disruption it prevents, but unreasonable when the hedge is disproportionately expensive. A hedge may also have non-financial costs, such as time, inconvenience, or reduced flexibility. The analysis should consider the coverage limits, exclusions, reliability, and whether the hedge actually addresses the identified risk. A hedge is not automatically worthwhile simply because it is available.
Study instruction:
- "Compare at least two hedging options using cost, event likelihood, potential impact, coverage, and residual exposure."
Key point:
- A hedge should be evaluated against the risk it actually reduces.
- The cost of protection should be proportionate to the consequence being mitigated.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
41. Q: Why might someone regret paying for a hedge that was never used, and how can hindsight bias affect that judgment?
A: Show answer
When a hedge is not used, the person may focus on its visible cost and conclude that the expense was unnecessary because the bad event did not occur. This is a hindsight trap: after learning the outcome, it can feel as though the event was obviously unlikely or that the protection was never needed. The chapter recommends considering this reaction in advance. The decision to buy a hedge should be judged using the information available at the time, including the risk and potential impact, not solely by whether the event later occurred. A sound hedge can be unused precisely because the adverse event did not happen; that does not by itself show that the original risk assessment was irrational.
Study instruction:
- "Before purchasing protection, write down the risk evidence and reasoning so that the decision can later be evaluated fairly."
Key point:
- An unused hedge is not automatically a wasted decision.
- Judge the choice by the information available when it was made, not only by the outcome.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
42. Q: How is hedging different from precommitment?
A: Show answer
A precommitment changes how a person is likely to act when a future temptation or emotional state appears. It may restrict an option, raise a barrier, lower a barrier, or specify a response in advance. A hedge, by contrast, reduces the impact of an external downside event and is often something the decision-maker pays for but hopes not to use. For example, setting a rule to wait before making an impulsive investment decision is a precommitment; maintaining insurance against a covered loss is a hedge. Some arrangements can have both characteristics, but the concepts answer different questions: precommitment addresses future behavior, while hedging addresses the consequences of adverse events.
Study instruction:
- "Classify three safeguards as precommitments, hedges, or both, and explain which risk each one addresses."
Key point:
- Precommitment protects the decision process from future impulses.
- Hedging protects against the impact of specified downside events.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
43. Q: How can a Decision Exploration Table guide changes to a plan before execution begins?
A: Show answer
The table can reveal that a goal is too ambitious, a decision depends on an unlikely assumption, or a foreseeable event could make the original approach unacceptable. The decision-maker may reduce scope, change timing, select a different option, add resources, or delay commitment until uncertainty is resolved. If the goal remains worthwhile, the table can identify actions that increase the chance of success and safeguards that reduce the impact of failure. The chapter's example of a possible regulatory ban illustrates why a team may decide not to enter a market until a major uncertainty is resolved. The decision to modify or stop should be based on the importance and credibility of the evidence, not on every imaginable risk.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one high-impact item in the table and document whether it requires changing scope, timing, resources, or the decision itself."
Key point:
- Risk analysis can justify changing the plan before resources are committed.
- Do not let an attractive goal prevent a rational response to new information.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
44. Q: How can a team turn premortem findings into accountable project controls?
A: Show answer
After the team identifies plausible failure causes, each material risk should be translated into an action or monitoring arrangement. The team can assign an owner, define an early warning indicator, set a review date, and agree on a response if the indicator appears. For example, a concern about a critical supplier may lead to a backup supplier, a delivery checkpoint, or a minimum inventory buffer. A concern about weak user adoption may lead to a pilot, training, or an adoption measure before full rollout. The team should record decisions and revisit the risk list as assumptions change. A premortem that ends with discussion but no owner, trigger, or response is unlikely to improve execution.
Study instruction:
- "Take three premortem findings and assign each an owner, indicator, deadline, and response."
Key point:
- A risk becomes manageable when it has an owner and a defined response.
- The team should revisit controls as project conditions change.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
45. Q: How should decision-makers respond when a premortem identifies a risk outside their control?
A: Show answer
A risk outside one's control should not be ignored simply because it cannot be prevented. The decision-maker can monitor leading indicators, reduce exposure, prepare a contingency, diversify dependencies, or purchase a hedge where appropriate. If the event would make the goal unacceptable or impossible, the decision may need to be delayed, modified, or abandoned. The chapter distinguishes causes outside the person's control from actions within the person's control, but this does not mean external risks are unmanageable. The relevant question is what influence the person has over exposure and consequences. A realistic response accepts uncertainty without pretending that every outcome can be controlled.
Study instruction:
- "For one external risk, identify an indicator, a contingency action, and a point at which the decision should be reconsidered."
Key point:
- Lack of control over an event does not eliminate the ability to prepare.
- Monitor, mitigate, hedge, or change course according to the risk's impact.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
46. Q: What is the difference between preparing for failure and expecting failure?
A: Show answer
Preparing for failure means identifying plausible obstacles so that the decision-maker can prevent, detect, or mitigate them. Expecting failure means treating a negative outcome as inevitable or allowing pessimism to replace analysis. The chapter's approach is not to lower ambition; it is to improve the route to a desired outcome by making hidden risks visible. A premortem should therefore generate testable concerns and practical responses. If the exercise produces only generalized fear, blame, or reasons to abandon every initiative, it has not been used constructively. Backcasting balances the analysis by identifying plausible success paths and the actions that support them.
Study instruction:
- "Review your risk list and remove vague fears that have no plausible mechanism, evidence, or actionable response."
Key point:
- Preparation treats failure as a possibility to manage, not a certainty to accept.
- Backcasting helps prevent risk analysis from becoming one-sided pessimism.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
47. Q: How can a decision-maker avoid confusing a good decision with a good outcome?
A: Show answer
A decision is made using information and uncertainty available at the time, while an outcome is what eventually happens. A sound process can produce a bad result because of external events, and a weak process can produce a good result through luck. The chapter's discussion of unexpectedly positive outcomes warns against treating success as proof that shortcuts are reliable. Its discussion of setbacks warns against allowing one bad outcome to trigger a chain of poor decisions. A careful review should ask whether the reasoning, evidence, assumptions, and safeguards were appropriate when the decision was made. This distinction helps preserve learning without relying solely on hindsight.
Study instruction:
- "Review one recent success and one setback, assessing the decision process separately from the final outcome."
Key point:
- Outcome alone does not reveal whether the decision process was sound.
- Learning requires examining information and reasoning available at the time.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
48. Q: How can the chapter's tools be combined into one practical decision process?
A: Show answer
Start with a clear goal or decision and a realistic time horizon. Conduct a premortem to identify failure causes within and outside your control, then conduct a backcast to identify success paths and enabling conditions. Combine the findings in a Decision Exploration Table and assess likelihood and impact. Decide whether to proceed, modify, delay, or stop. If proceeding, create precommitments that raise barriers to harmful behavior and lower barriers to useful behavior. Use the Dr. Evil game to identify recurring exceptions and adopt category decisions where appropriate. Prepare a tilt inventory and a response to likely setbacks, including criteria for changing course. Finally, consider hedges for external downside events and review the plan as evidence changes.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the full sequence to one consequential decision and document the decision, safeguards, owners, and review points."
Key point:
- The tools form a connected process from anticipation to action and review.
- The purpose is to improve execution and resilience, not to eliminate uncertainty.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
- Klusmann et al.: Mental Contrasting and Behavior Change
- Gollwitzer and Sheeran: Implementation Intentions
- Staw: The Escalation of Commitment
49. Q: What would a complete premortem and backcast look like for a major project launch?
A: Show answer
A team launching a new service could define success as reaching an agreed adoption and service-quality target within a year. In the premortem, members imagine that the launch failed and list reasons such as poor user research, unrealistic timing, insufficient training, integration defects, weak support capacity, or a competitor's move. They separate internal execution causes from external events. In the backcast, they imagine the launch succeeded and identify actions such as a controlled pilot, early user feedback, realistic resourcing, clear ownership, and reliable support. The Decision Exploration Table then ranks the likelihood and impact of these factors. The team may change scope, add a pilot, secure backup capacity, or define conditions for delaying release.
Study instruction:
- "Create a launch scenario with at least four failure causes, four success conditions, and three resulting plan changes."
Key point:
- The project plan should reflect both failure pathways and success enablers.
- A premortem is useful when it changes launch decisions, controls, or contingency plans.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
50. Q: How can the Dr. Evil game be used in a team without turning it into a blame exercise?
A: Show answer
The facilitator should frame the game around patterns of decision-making rather than personal character. Team members can identify small choices that would gradually defeat the team's goal, such as repeatedly accepting unapproved scope changes, postponing difficult conversations, ignoring quality warnings, or making exceptions to a procurement control. The group should examine why each choice would appear reasonable in isolation and how its cumulative effect would become harmful. The discussion should then identify category rules, escalation triggers, or review points that make the pattern visible. No participant should be singled out as the imagined villain. The exercise is intended to reveal vulnerabilities in routines and incentives, not to assign blame for hypothetical failure.
Study instruction:
- "Run the exercise using process-level examples and agree on one category rule or monitoring measure for each recurring risk."
Key point:
- The game should expose patterns and system weaknesses, not label individuals.
- A useful output is a preventive rule or monitoring control.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
51. Q: What does the chapter's Darth Vader example illustrate about overconfidence and dissent?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the fictional Death Star story to illustrate the danger of treating a powerful plan or technology as invulnerable. The commander dismisses the possibility that the Rebels could exploit a weakness, while Vader recognizes that a seemingly secure system may contain a fatal vulnerability. The point is not to imitate Vader's abusive behavior, which the chapter explicitly treats as unsuitable leadership. The lesson is that a leader should invite credible challenges and examine failure paths before an adversary or external event exposes them. A confident team may be especially vulnerable when it interprets dissent as disloyalty or assumes that technical superiority removes the need for contingency planning.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one assumption in a plan that has been treated as unquestionable and ask what evidence could disprove it."
Key point:
- Confidence can become dangerous when it suppresses examination of vulnerabilities.
- Leaders should invite dissent without using intimidation or humiliation.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
- Gary Klein: Performing a Project Premortem
52. Q: What does the Dr. Evil fourth-down example show about repeated conservative decisions?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes football coaches who may override analytics in favor of a conservative choice, such as kicking rather than going for it on fourth down. A single override may have a plausible in-game explanation, such as momentum or a weakness in the offensive line. The pattern becomes more revealing when a coach repeatedly chooses the conservative option in close situations, even when the analysis favors another choice. This illustrates how an apparently reasonable one-off decision can conceal a systematic bias when viewed across many decisions. The lesson is to examine the pattern and the decision rule, not just the justification offered for one instance.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a recurring decision and compare actual choices with the stated decision criteria across multiple instances."
Key point:
- Patterns can reveal bias that individual justifications conceal.
- Review repeated decisions against consistent criteria rather than isolated explanations.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
53. Q: What practical checklist summarizes the chapter's method for improving future execution?
A: Show answer
The chapter's checklist asks the reader to conduct a premortem and a backcast, combine the results in a Decision Exploration Table with likelihood estimates, and consider whether the goal or decision should change. If proceeding, the reader should identify precommitment contracts, plan responses to failure causes, play the Dr. Evil game to uncover repeated self-sabotaging choices, consider category decisions, and assess possible hedges against bad luck. The sequence matters because safeguards should respond to identified risks rather than be selected randomly. The checklist is a repeatable review process for decisions that depend on future execution. It should be adapted to the importance, uncertainty, and reversibility of the specific decision.
Study instruction:
- "Use the checklist before one future decision and record which steps changed your plan or behavior."
Key point:
- The chapter combines anticipation, planning, commitment, and risk mitigation.
- Use the tools proportionately and focus on decisions where future execution matters.

- Penguin Random House: How to Decide
