Table 9
Decision Hygiene and Unbiased Feedback
1. Q: What is decision hygiene, and why does Chapter 9 compare it with handwashing?
A: Show answer
Decision hygiene is the deliberate use of procedures that protect the information and feedback used in decisions from avoidable contamination by the decision-maker's beliefs, preferred conclusions, framing, and knowledge of outcomes. The chapter compares this discipline with handwashing because the same logic applies in both cases: an unseen contaminant can be transferred from one setting to another unless a deliberate safeguard interrupts the transfer. In the chapter's historical example, Ignaz Semmelweis introduced handwashing after connecting doctors' contact with autopsy material to childbed fever. The decision-making analogy is that broadcasting a belief before asking for feedback can alter what another person thinks or is willing to say. Hygiene therefore concerns the process used to obtain information, not a guarantee that every later decision will succeed.
Study instruction:
- "Study the handwashing analogy and be able to explain what is being transferred, how contamination occurs, and which process safeguards reduce it."
Key point:
- Decision hygiene protects the independence and usefulness of feedback.
- A sound process reduces contamination but cannot guarantee a favourable outcome.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
2. Q: What does the Semmelweis example contribute to the chapter's argument about decision hygiene?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Semmelweis's observation that childbed-fever mortality fell from 16% to 2% after he introduced handwashing in the obstetrics ward. It recounts how his superiors resisted the implication that doctors' practices contributed to deaths, partly because the proposal challenged their self-image. The example illustrates motivated reasoning: people may discount relevant evidence when accepting it threatens a valued belief or identity. Duke uses the story as an analogy, not as a claim that medical infection and belief influence are identical phenomena. The transferable lesson is to build safeguards that do not depend entirely on people recognizing their own bias in the moment. A process can be necessary precisely because good intentions and confidence do not prevent contamination.
Study instruction:
- "Recall the before-and-after mortality figures given in the chapter and distinguish the historical example from the decision-hygiene analogy drawn from it."
Key point:
- The chapter reports a fall in mortality from 16% to 2% after handwashing was introduced.
- Motivated reasoning can obstruct acceptance of evidence that threatens identity or status.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
3. Q: What is belief contagion in the context of seeking feedback?
A: Show answer
Belief contagion occurs when the person requesting feedback reveals their own view before hearing another person's view, increasing the chance that the other person's response will move toward the requester's position or be expressed as agreement. The effect can occur even when the requester does not intend to influence anyone. A person may want to be agreeable, avoid embarrassment, or avoid creating friction. As a result, the feedback may reflect social accommodation rather than the person's independent assessment. This is especially damaging when the purpose of asking is to discover what the requester does not already know. The resulting answer can look like independent confirmation while actually being influenced by the way the question was asked.
Study instruction:
- "Practice identifying when a request for feedback reveals the seeker's preferred answer before the respondent has given an independent view."
Key point:
- The order in which information is disclosed can change the feedback received.
- Apparent agreement is not necessarily independent confirmation.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
4. Q: How does the chapter's opinion-elicitation experiment test whether beliefs are contagious?
A: Show answer
The exercise asks the reader to select a topic on which people are likely to hold different views and record their own opinion first. One group is asked for opinions after hearing the reader's view; another group is asked without first being told that view. The reader records each group's responses and compares the level of agreement, including whether people in the second group ask to hear the reader's opinion before answering. The chapter predicts that the group exposed to the reader's opinion will usually show more agreement. The experiment is a practical demonstration for reflection, not a controlled scientific estimate of the size of the effect. Its purpose is to make the influence of disclosure order observable in everyday feedback seeking.
Study instruction:
- "Run the exercise using a topic with a genuine range of views and keep the wording of the two requests as similar as possible apart from disclosure of your opinion."
Key point:
- Compare responses elicited before and after disclosure of the requester's view.
- Treat the exercise as a demonstration, not as a population-level experiment.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
5. Q: Why might people agree with a requester even when they hold a different private opinion?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies social and psychological reasons for expressed agreement. A respondent may not want to embarrass the requester, create conflict, appear difficult, or expose themselves to embarrassment if they think their own view is mistaken. The respondent may also ask what the requester thinks before answering so that they can avoid an overt disagreement. These pressures can affect what people say even if their underlying belief has not changed. Consequently, a decision-maker should not interpret a polite or unanimous response as proof that the group shares the same independent assessment. Feedback methods should give people a way to state a view before learning which answer is socially safer or more acceptable.
Study instruction:
- "When reviewing feedback, distinguish a person's privately held view from the view they felt comfortable expressing in the conversation."
Key point:
- Silence or expressed agreement can conceal disagreement.
- A good feedback process makes independent responses easier to give.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
6. Q: What does the chapter say about Solomon Asch's line-judgment experiment?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Asch's conformity experiment in which participants judged which comparison line matched a reference line. When people made the judgment independently, the chapter reports that more than 99% selected the correct line. In the group condition, confederates deliberately gave the same incorrect answer before the participant responded. The chapter reports that 36.8% of actual experimental subjects went along with the incorrect group answer. The result illustrates that social influence can affect even a seemingly straightforward perceptual judgment. It should not be restated as meaning that every participant conformed or that people always abandon their own judgment. The relevant lesson is that group context can influence expressed answers, even when the task appears objectively clear.
Study instruction:
- "Review the distinction between independent judgments and judgments made after hearing a unanimous group response; remember what the 36.8% figure measures."
Key point:
- The chapter reports a 36.8% error rate on critical trials under group pressure.
- Social influence can affect expressed judgments without implying universal conformity.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- The Power of Social Influence: A Replication and Extension of the Asch Experiment
7. Q: Why does the chapter use the idea of two maps to explain divergent beliefs?
A: Show answer
The map metaphor treats each person as having a mental map of facts, experiences, and opinions. Some parts of two people's maps overlap, while other parts diverge. People naturally notice and seek the overlap because agreement feels comfortable, but divergence may reveal information one person lacks or an assumption that needs testing. The chapter argues that the useful information often lies in the places where the maps differ. Exploring those differences can correct incomplete knowledge, refine estimates, or expose the limits of a person's reasoning. The metaphor does not mean that every differing opinion is equally accurate. It means that divergence is a cue to investigate rather than an automatic reason to dismiss or defend a position.
Study instruction:
- "Draw two maps for a real decision: list shared facts, different assumptions, and information possessed by only one person."
Key point:
- Overlap shows agreement; divergence can reveal corrective information.
- A difference of opinion is an opportunity to investigate, not proof of who is right.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
8. Q: What three possibilities should a decision-maker consider when another person's view diverges from their own?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three possibilities. First, when two people are similarly informed but reach opposite conclusions, the objective truth may lie between their positions, so both can moderate their beliefs. Second, the requester may be wrong and the other person right, in which case listening can correct an inaccurate belief that would otherwise weaken future decisions. Third, the requester may be right and the other person wrong, but explaining the reasoning can still improve the requester's understanding and expose gaps in the argument. These possibilities show why divergence can benefit both participants. A decision-maker should not assume that disagreement means the other person is uninformed, hostile, or simply mistaken.
Study instruction:
- "For a disagreement you encounter, write down all three possibilities before deciding whether to defend, revise, or investigate your view."
Key point:
- Divergence can reveal a midpoint, correct the requester, or strengthen a well-founded view.
- Both sides can learn even when only one side changes its conclusion.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
9. Q: How can a person benefit when their original belief is correct and the other person is wrong?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that a correct belief can become better understood through the act of explaining it. A person who must present reasons rather than rely on familiar slogans or authority may discover which parts of the argument are strong, which are incomplete, and what evidence supports the conclusion. The exercise involving a flat-Earth claim asks the reader to explain why the Earth is round without relying on parental authority, consensus, or unexplained images. The point is not to endorse the false claim; it is to test whether the reader understands the grounds for the correct belief. Explaining a position can therefore improve clarity and make the belief more robust for future decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a belief you regard as well established and practise explaining its evidence without relying only on authority or familiar slogans."
Key point:
- Explaining a correct belief can deepen understanding of why it is correct.
- A conclusion supported by reasons is more useful than a conclusion held by habit alone.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
10. Q: Why should a person keep their opinion to themselves when first requesting feedback?
A: Show answer
Withholding the requester's opinion allows the respondent to give an initial assessment without knowing which answer would agree with or challenge the requester. This reduces two risks described in the chapter: the respondent may change their belief after hearing the requester's view, or may retain their belief but suppress it when expressing the answer. The requester should still provide relevant facts needed to assess the question, but should avoid disclosing their preferred answer or action. For example, someone seeking advice about a poker hand can explain the cards, betting order, and stack sizes without saying what they actually did. The aim is to obtain the respondent's independent judgment before the requester's position becomes a social signal.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a feedback request so that it contains the decision facts but not your current preference or chosen action."
Key point:
- Provide necessary facts while withholding your own conclusion.
- The first response is more informative when it is not anchored to the requester's view.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
11. Q: What are the two ways that telling someone your opinion first can contaminate their feedback?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes two distinct mechanisms. The first is belief movement: hearing the requester's position can cause the respondent's opinion to bend toward it, sometimes without the respondent noticing the change. The second is expression suppression: the respondent's belief may remain unchanged, but they may avoid stating it because they do not want to embarrass the requester, create friction, or look disagreeable. These mechanisms require different interpretations. A changed opinion and a concealed opinion can both produce apparent agreement, but neither gives the requester a clean sample of the respondent's starting view. Keeping the requester's position undisclosed reduces the opportunity for either mechanism to affect the initial response.
Study instruction:
- "For each example of apparent agreement, ask whether the respondent may have changed their view or merely chosen not to express disagreement."
Key point:
- Feedback can be contaminated by changing a belief or suppressing its expression.
- A neutral first question protects the respondent's independent starting point.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
12. Q: How should a requester distinguish necessary background facts from their own opinion?
A: Show answer
Necessary background facts are the information the respondent needs to evaluate the decision; the requester's opinion is the conclusion, preference, or action the requester has already adopted. In the chapter's poker example, the order of betting, players' chip stacks, and opponents' playing styles are relevant facts. The requester's actual action in the hand is an opinion-laden disclosure that can anchor the response. A useful test is to ask what information would be needed to evaluate the situation if the respondent had no knowledge of the requester's eventual choice. Facts should be sufficient for analysis, but not arranged to steer the answer toward a preferred conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Separate a feedback brief into verified situation facts, missing information, and the conclusion or action you are deliberately withholding."
Key point:
- Give facts needed for analysis, not the answer you hope to hear.
- The line between facts and opinion should be considered before asking for advice.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
13. Q: What does the chapter mean when it says outcomes can also contaminate feedback?
A: Show answer
Knowing how a decision turned out can influence how another person evaluates the decision that came before it. A successful outcome may make the decision appear wiser than it was, while a poor outcome may make it appear worse. This is outcome contamination because the evaluator receives information that was unavailable to the decision-maker at the time. The chapter connects this to resulting and hindsight bias. When asking whether a past decision was well made, the requester should avoid revealing the actual result at first. The evaluator should assess the decision using the facts and uncertainty that existed when it was made, rather than allowing the eventual result to cast a shadow over the earlier judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a past-decision review that presents the decision context before disclosing what happened afterward."
Key point:
- An outcome can distort an assessment of the decision process that preceded it.
- Evaluate the choice using information available at the time, not hindsight.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
14. Q: How does withholding the outcome help prevent resulting and hindsight bias?
A: Show answer
The chapter says that if an evaluator does not know the outcome, the outcome cannot drive the evaluation in the same way. Without the result, the evaluator is less able to treat success as proof of good judgment or failure as proof of bad judgment, and less likely to regard the result as predictable after the fact. This does not eliminate every bias or guarantee an accurate evaluation. It removes one powerful source of contamination. The chapter notes that people often find this difficult because the result feels relevant to the advice request. It can be relevant in a large enough sample, but for one decision with one particular result, knowing the result can distort the review of the earlier choice.
Study instruction:
- "In a retrospective review, record the evaluator's assessment before revealing the result and compare whether the assessment changes afterward."
Key point:
- Withholding the result helps keep the evaluation focused on the pre-result decision.
- A single realised outcome is not a complete measure of decision quality.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
15. Q: What is iterative feedback, and when does the chapter recommend using it?
A: Show answer
Iterative feedback is a staged method for reviewing a decision that contains several decision points or later outcomes. Instead of telling the whole story at once, the requester presents the information available before the first decision and asks for feedback. After recording the response, the requester reveals the next stage's information and asks for feedback before disclosing the next choice or result. In the hiring example, the evaluator first considers the job description and salary range, then the hiring method, then the finalist information, with the requester's actions and outcomes withheld until each relevant assessment has been made. The sequence helps preserve the evaluator's state of knowledge at each stage.
Study instruction:
- "For a multi-stage decision, create a timeline and prepare one feedback question for each decision point before revealing the next stage."
Key point:
- Iterate feedback when multiple choices and outcomes would otherwise be disclosed together.
- Pause the narrative before each decision result so the evaluator can assess the information available then.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
16. Q: What does it mean to put a feedback provider in the decision-maker's original state of knowledge?
A: Show answer
It means reconstructing, as closely as possible, the facts, uncertainties, options, and constraints that were available when the original decision was made. A fair reviewer should not be given later discoveries, the eventual result, or facts that the decision-maker could not reasonably have known at the time. The chapter explains that decision records such as decision trees, a Knowledge Tracker, and a Decision Exploration Table can preserve this information. Such records make it easier to obtain useful feedback later without allowing hindsight to rewrite the original information set. Perfect reconstruction may not always be possible, but the objective is to avoid evaluating an earlier choice with knowledge acquired afterward.
Study instruction:
- "For an important decision, keep a time-stamped record of the information, assumptions, alternatives, and uncertainties that informed it."
Key point:
- Fair feedback requires recreating the original information set.
- Contemporaneous records make later review more reliable.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
17. Q: How can the wording of a feedback question signal the answer the requester prefers?
A: Show answer
A question can signal a preferred conclusion through loaded wording, labels, assumptions, and emotional tone. The chapter's example contrasts asking what someone thinks about Kevin with asking whether Kevin is a total jerk. The latter embeds a negative assessment in the question, making agreement easier and disagreement more socially awkward. Respondents may react to the frame rather than independently assess the person or situation. A requester should therefore review the wording for implied judgments, leading assumptions, and emotionally charged labels. Neutral wording does not remove all bias, but it reduces unnecessary signals about the answer the requester hopes to receive.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a leading question into a neutral version that allows positive, negative, mixed, and uncertain responses."
Key point:
- The question itself can influence the response.
- Neutral wording reduces the signal about the requester's preferred conclusion.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
18. Q: What is the framing effect, as defined in Chapter 9?
A: Show answer
The chapter defines the framing effect as a cognitive bias in which the way information is presented influences how the listener makes decisions about that information. Different descriptions can lead people to evaluate the same underlying issue differently. In feedback seeking, a frame can signal whether the requester views a person, action, or proposal positively or negatively. For example, a question that labels a colleague as difficult already encourages the listener to interpret the colleague through that negative description. The decision-hygiene response is to use a neutral frame, describe observable circumstances, and let the respondent form an initial assessment before hearing the requester's own conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Identify loaded adjectives, assumptions, and one-sided descriptions in feedback requests and replace them with observable, neutral language."
Key point:
- Presentation can shape evaluation even when the underlying topic is unchanged.
- Neutral framing helps the respondent assess the issue rather than the requester's label.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
19. Q: Why does the chapter prefer the terms divergence or dispersion of opinion to disagreement?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that the word disagreement can carry negative social connotations, while divergence and dispersion describe differences more neutrally. Calling someone disagreeable is often understood as criticism of the person, not simply a description of different views. More neutral terms make it easier to discuss where opinions differ without implying hostility or personal defect. This vocabulary can help a group treat differences as information to explore rather than as conflict to suppress. The terms do not change the substance of the difference; they change the language used to describe it and can reduce an unnecessary emotional signal during feedback discussions.
Study instruction:
- "When facilitating a review, describe points of divergence and ask what information or assumptions explain them."
Key point:
- Neutral vocabulary makes differences easier to examine.
- A difference of view need not be framed as a personal conflict.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
20. Q: Why is belief contagion especially difficult to manage in a group discussion?
A: Show answer
In a one-to-one exchange, the requester can keep their view private until the respondent answers. In a group, however, once one member speaks, everyone else hears that view and may be influenced by it. A rapid consensus can then create social pressure for later speakers to align with the emerging majority. Members holding useful but divergent information may remain silent, so the group hears a narrower picture than the members collectively possess. The chapter warns that groups may gain confidence from involving more people even when the discussion has not improved the underlying decision. The challenge is to collect independent views before group interaction creates a shared anchor.
Study instruction:
- "Before a group meeting, design a process for capturing each member's initial view and rationale independently."
Key point:
- The first public opinion can influence every later contribution.
- More participants can increase confidence without improving information quality.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
21. Q: What is the difference between a group's information capacity and the information it actually uses?
A: Show answer
A group may collectively possess enough facts and expertise to reach a strong decision, yet fail to bring those facts into the discussion or final choice. The chapter's hidden-profile example shows that individual members held different pieces of candidate information. Although the combined information favoured Candidate A, the partial-information groups often reached a different conclusion because members did not share information that challenged the emerging consensus. Information capacity is what the members collectively know; information use is what becomes visible, is discussed, and influences the decision. Good group design must bridge this gap rather than assume that putting knowledgeable people in one room automatically pools their knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "For a committee decision, inventory what each member uniquely knows and check that the final discussion explicitly considers those facts."
Key point:
- Collective knowledge is not useful if it is not surfaced and considered.
- A group process must deliberately pool unique information.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- Stasser and Titus, Pooling of Unshared Information in Group Decision Making (1985)
22. Q: What is a hidden profile in group decision-making?
A: Show answer
A hidden profile occurs when information is distributed across group members so that no one person's initial information set reveals the best alternative, but the combined information would support that alternative if it were pooled. In the chapter's candidate-selection example, each person in a partial-information group received only part of the candidate dossiers. The complete set of facts collectively favoured Candidate A, but the group's initial preferences tended to favour other candidates. Because discussion converged early, members did not adequately surface information that would have changed the group's assessment. The concept demonstrates why unique facts must be actively elicited rather than assumed to emerge naturally through unstructured discussion.
Study instruction:
- "Use a hiring or investment committee example to identify which critical facts are shared and which are held by only one member."
Key point:
- A hidden profile is a best alternative supported by information dispersed across members.
- The group must surface unshared information to discover the stronger option.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- Stasser and Titus, Pooling of Unshared Information in Group Decision Making (1985)
23. Q: What did the Stasser and Titus candidate-selection study illustrate?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes four-person groups choosing among three candidates for student body president. In the complete-information condition, members had full dossiers and Candidate A was generally the preferred option. In the partial-information condition, members received incomplete dossiers with common facts and other facts distributed among members. Although pooling the information would have revealed Candidate A as the most favourable candidate, the partial-information groups tended to form consensus around their premeeting preferences and rarely shared the divergent facts needed to correct that view. The study illustrates how discussion can favour information already shared or supporting an emerging preference over unique information that could change the decision.
Study instruction:
- "Read the study's basic design and explain why the partial-information groups could reach a poorer group choice despite collectively holding the relevant facts."
Key point:
- The study showed a failure to pool information that was distributed across members.
- Early consensus can discourage the disclosure of facts that challenge it.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- Stasser and Titus, Pooling of Unshared Information in Group Decision Making (1985)
24. Q: Why can a group become more confident without becoming more accurate?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that group participation can make a decision feel more robust simply because many people have been involved. But if members repeat shared information, align with the first speakers, or withhold facts that conflict with consensus, the number of participants does not guarantee a better information base. Confidence may rise as the group converges even though the discussion has not tested alternatives or combined unique knowledge. This creates a dangerous mismatch between confidence and decision quality. Groups should therefore evaluate the quality of the process, including whether divergent information was heard, rather than using unanimity or the number of participants as a proxy for accuracy.
Study instruction:
- "After a group decision, review whether confidence is supported by independently gathered evidence and explicit consideration of contrary information."
Key point:
- Consensus and confidence are not substitutes for accurate information pooling.
- The decision process should be assessed separately from the feeling of agreement.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- Stasser and Titus, Pooling of Unshared Information in Group Decision Making (1985)
25. Q: How can a group solicit initial opinions independently before discussion?
A: Show answer
Each member should submit an initial opinion and its rationale privately before anyone discusses their view with the rest of the group. The submissions can be collected by email, a secure form, or another private method, then compiled and shared before the meeting. The chapter gives examples involving a hiring committee, an investment committee, and a legal team assessing settlement ranges. The key is to preserve each member's independent starting point and make the range of reasoning visible before the group discussion can create an anchor. The process should specify when responses are due, what information members receive, and what form the response should take.
Study instruction:
- "For the next committee decision, circulate the same neutral brief to every member and collect written views before scheduling the group discussion."
Key point:
- Independent first responses protect the diversity of views before discussion.
- Written rationales make it easier to identify both agreement and divergence.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
26. Q: Why does the chapter recommend collecting rationales as well as conclusions?
A: Show answer
A conclusion alone tells the group what a member prefers but not why. The rationale reveals which facts, assumptions, estimates, values, or risks produced that preference. Seeing rationales can expose unique information, differences in interpretation, and gaps in understanding that would be invisible in a simple vote. It also helps the group compare like with like and investigate the reasons for divergence rather than immediately debating personalities or positions. The chapter encourages specifying the form of feedback sought, such as a forecast, a decision-tree outcome, a range, a yes-or-no judgment, or a rating scale, so contributions can be compared more systematically.
Study instruction:
- "Require each initial response to include a recommendation, the key reasons supporting it, and any material uncertainty or missing fact."
Key point:
- Rationales reveal the information and assumptions behind a position.
- A defined response format improves comparison across group members.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
27. Q: How does private electronic feedback differ from public hand-raising?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research in which students who raised their hands publicly tended to follow a consensus as it formed, creating larger apparent majorities. When students responded with electronic clickers and could not see how others were answering, the super-majorities diminished. The contrast illustrates that the visibility and order of responses can change the distribution of expressed views. Private electronic feedback does not guarantee truth or careful reasoning, but it reduces the immediate social cue created by watching others answer. A group seeking independent views should therefore consider private collection methods when public responses might produce herding.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a private response method when participants can see one another's answers and might be influenced by the developing majority."
Key point:
- Publicly visible responses can create a herding effect.
- Private initial feedback can better reveal the spread of knowledge and preferences.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
28. Q: What does it mean to specify the form of feedback a group is seeking?
A: Show answer
Specifying the form means telling members what output to provide before they submit their view. The chapter lists possible formats such as forecasts of events, decision-tree outcomes, options, payoffs, counterfactuals, a Perspective Tracker, a Decision Exploration Table, premortem or backcast outputs, yes-or-no answers, and rating scales. A defined format makes responses easier to compare and reduces the risk that one member gives a broad impression while another provides a detailed analysis. The appropriate form depends on the decision. For a candidate selection, it might be a ranked choice with rationale; for a legal settlement, it might be a recommended amount, upper and lower bounds, and an estimated likelihood of acceptance.
Study instruction:
- "Before collecting views, define the response fields, scale, range, or decision tool that will make the answers comparable."
Key point:
- A specified output format supports apples-to-apples comparison.
- The format should match the decision being evaluated.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
29. Q: Why does the chapter recommend anonymizing the first round of group feedback?
A: Show answer
Anonymizing the first round separates the content of a view from the identity and status of the person who expressed it. The chapter notes that ideas from senior or highly visible individuals can be especially contagious, while ideas from junior members may receive less attention regardless of merit. A first anonymous review encourages the group to consider the reasoning before reacting to the source. This can help surface less familiar or unconventional perspectives and reduce the pressure to agree publicly with a powerful participant. Anonymity is an initial protection rather than a permanent rule: once the ideas have been considered on their merits, expertise and accountability can be considered where relevant.
Study instruction:
- "Collect views without names, circulate the combined set for an initial merits-based review, and reveal attribution only when it is useful and appropriate."
Key point:
- Anonymity helps protect initial feedback from status-based influence.
- Consider the quality of the idea before considering who expressed it.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
30. Q: What is the halo effect, and how can it distort group feedback?
A: Show answer
The chapter defines the halo effect as a cognitive bias in which a positive impression of a person in one area produces a positive view of that person in other, unrelated areas. In a group, a successful leader, expert, or persuasive speaker may be given extra weight even when the topic falls outside that person's expertise. Other members may hesitate to challenge that person, allowing status to influence the discussion beyond the person's actual knowledge of the subject. The chapter uses an exaggerated example of people being reluctant to contradict Einstein even on a matter unrelated to physics. Anonymizing the first round helps separate the initial merits of an idea from the prestige of its source.
Study instruction:
- "When evaluating a proposal, list the evidence supporting it before recording the presenter's title, seniority, or reputation."
Key point:
- A person's reputation in one field can spill over into unrelated judgments.
- Status should not replace topic-relevant evidence or expertise.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
31. Q: Should feedback remain anonymous throughout the entire decision process?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter argues for anonymizing the first pass, not keeping authorship hidden forever. Expertise and experience can be relevant to how much weight a group gives a particular assessment. For example, a physicist's view may deserve special consideration when the group is deciding a physics question. At the same time, a highly regarded expert can still have biases or lack relevant expertise outside their field. The sequence matters: first let ideas receive consideration on their merits without the status cue; later, once the group understands the substance, it can consider each contributor's relevant knowledge, experience, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Design the process so that anonymity protects the first assessment but does not prevent later verification of expertise and responsibility."
Key point:
- Anonymity is a first-pass safeguard, not a claim that expertise is irrelevant.
- Weigh expertise only where it is relevant to the subject being decided.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
32. Q: Why does the chapter warn that subject-matter experts are not immune to bias?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that expertise can provide valuable knowledge while also making a person more entrenched in a familiar worldview. A strong mental model can make it harder to see an issue from a perspective that challenges that model. The chapter refers to Philip Tetlock's work on experts to support the warning that specialist status does not eliminate bias. In a group, this means that an expert's contribution should be taken seriously for relevant knowledge but not treated as automatically complete or beyond question. Anonymity at the first stage allows the group to examine the content of different views before expertise or prestige affects how much attention each one receives.
Study instruction:
- "For an important expert recommendation, identify the evidence, assumptions, and plausible counterarguments before deciding how much weight to give it."
Key point:
- Expertise is valuable but does not make a person bias-free.
- A strong worldview can make alternative perspectives harder to recognize.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
33. Q: What does the chapter mean by giving junior members a chance to be heard?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that people with lower status may possess different information or see options that more senior members overlook. They may also be less anchored to the existing way of doing things. If a senior person's opinion is expressed first, junior members can become reluctant to share a contrary view. A structured first round gives everyone a chance to contribute before status cues shape the discussion. The chapter does not claim that junior members are always correct or unusually insightful; their contributions may also reveal gaps in understanding. The aim is to prevent status from deciding in advance which ideas receive attention.
Study instruction:
- "Invite every member to submit an independent view before the chair or most senior participant speaks."
Key point:
- Lower-status members may hold information or perspectives the group needs.
- Equal opportunity to contribute is not the same as assuming every contribution is equally correct.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
34. Q: What is the chapter's 'But-Why'd' idea, and why is it useful?
A: Show answer
The 'But-Why'd' idea describes the value of allowing less experienced members to ask repeated questions about why something is true or why a recommendation follows. The chapter compares this to a child repeatedly asking a parent why the sky is blue. The questions can expose where an expert's explanation stops, which assumptions are being taken for granted, or where the expert's own understanding is incomplete. They can also create an opportunity for experts to explain their reasoning and transfer knowledge to others. In a group, a question that initially sounds elementary may therefore improve clarity for everyone and reveal a weak step in the argument.
Study instruction:
- "In a review meeting, invite clarifying 'why' questions and ask presenters to explain the reasoning behind each important assumption."
Key point:
- Basic questions can expose hidden assumptions and limits in expert understanding.
- Explaining reasoning can strengthen understanding across the group.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
35. Q: What is the quick-and-dirty group feedback method for lower-impact decisions?
A: Show answer
For lower-impact, easier-to-reverse decisions, the chapter proposes a shorter method that still protects initial opinions. Each person writes down a view and rationale; one person then reads the responses aloud or puts them on a whiteboard before open discussion begins. Another variation is for members to read their own written views aloud, starting with the most junior member and moving upward in seniority. These approaches require less administration than collecting and anonymizing email responses, while still giving participants a chance to state an initial view before hearing the most influential voices. The method is not presented as sufficient for every high-stakes or hard-to-reverse decision.
Study instruction:
- "Use written first responses for routine reversible choices, and preserve more rigorous independent collection for decisions with greater impact."
Key point:
- A brief written round can reduce contagion without a complex process.
- For the read-aloud version, start with the most junior member.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
36. Q: How should a team choose between a rigorous feedback process and a quick method?
A: Show answer
The chapter recognizes a time-accuracy trade-off. Independent private submissions, anonymous compilation, and structured review take time but provide stronger safeguards against group contagion. For lower-impact decisions that are easy to reverse, a quick written round or junior-first speaking order may be proportionate. The selection should reflect the consequences of error, reversibility, the need for diverse information, and the time needed to collect reliable input. The chapter does not prescribe a universal numerical threshold. A team should use the more rigorous process when a poor choice would be costly or difficult to undo, and a lighter process when the downside is limited and correction is straightforward.
Study instruction:
- "Before choosing the process, rate the decision's impact, reversibility, and information complexity, then document why the selected method is proportionate."
Key point:
- The depth of the process should reflect impact and reversibility.
- Speed should not be purchased at the cost of essential information for high-consequence decisions.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
37. Q: What is the chapter's 'Spin Doctrine' problem when requesting advice?
A: Show answer
The Spin Doctrine describes the way a requester can shape a narrative to make their preferred conclusion seem more persuasive. People may emphasize facts that support their view, minimize inconvenient details, or omit information that would lead the adviser to a different assessment. The chapter says this is often not a deliberate attempt to deceive someone else; it can be a way of fooling oneself. The result is that the adviser receives a distorted input and is more likely to notice the parts of the situation that overlap with the requester's existing map. Even a perfectly independent adviser cannot produce high-quality feedback from incomplete or slanted information.
Study instruction:
- "Review an advice brief for emphasized, minimized, and omitted facts before sending it to the adviser."
Key point:
- Feedback quality is limited by the quality of the information supplied.
- Narrative spin can be self-serving even when the requester does not intend to mislead.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
38. Q: Why is 'junk in, junk out' relevant even when feedback is obtained independently?
A: Show answer
Independent feedback protects against social influence, but it cannot compensate for missing or distorted facts. If an adviser is not told that a job candidate was convicted of embezzlement, or that a lawsuit has been reassigned to a judge with a relevant reputation, the advice may be poor even if the adviser has no knowledge of the requester's preference. The chapter's point is that quarantine and completeness are separate requirements. A good process must both protect the independence of the response and provide enough relevant information for the respondent to assess the decision. The adviser should also be able to ask for omitted facts before giving a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Check whether an independent adviser has received all material facts before treating their response as reliable."
Key point:
- Independent opinions based on incomplete inputs can still be misleading.
- Decision hygiene requires both unbiased elicitation and adequate information.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
39. Q: How can a decision-maker use the outside view to improve a feedback request?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends temporarily putting oneself in the position of the person being asked for advice. The requester should ask: if someone approached me about this type of decision, what would I need to know to give high-quality feedback? This question helps identify facts that the requester may overlook because they are already invested in a preferred option. It moves attention away from the requester's narrative and toward the information a neutral evaluator would require. The resulting list becomes a checklist for preparing future advice requests. The outside view is not a replacement for understanding the specific case; it is a way to identify the information needed to assess it fairly.
Study instruction:
- "Imagine advising another person facing the same category of decision and list the information you would request before offering an opinion."
Key point:
- The outside view helps identify what an adviser needs rather than what the requester wants to emphasize.
- Prepare the information checklist before becoming attached to a particular option.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
40. Q: What information should a feedback checklist normally contain?
A: Show answer
The chapter says the checklist will vary with the decision but commonly includes goals, values, resources, and the details of the situation. Goals explain what the person is trying to accomplish. Values identify what matters in choosing among outcomes. Resources and constraints define what is feasible. Situation details provide the facts needed to assess the alternatives. In a vacation example, a person who wants sunshine and history may also need to disclose that only three days are available; without that constraint, the advice may be unsuitable. The checklist should include what the adviser needs to know and no more, so the brief is complete without being overloaded with irrelevant material.
Study instruction:
- "For one recurring decision, build a checklist starting with goals, values, resources, and constraints, then add the case-specific facts."
Key point:
- Relevant goals, values, resources, and situational details shape what advice is useful.
- A checklist should be complete but limited to information that matters to the decision.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
41. Q: Why should a checklist be created before a specific decision is underway?
A: Show answer
Once a person is in the middle of a decision, they may already prefer one option. That preference can influence which facts they consider relevant and which details they disclose. Creating the checklist in advance reduces the influence of the current case and makes it easier to identify relevant information objectively. A prebuilt checklist is particularly useful for recurring decisions such as hiring, investment assessment, or other repeated choices. It also creates consistency: different requests can be prepared using the same baseline information requirements, making feedback easier to compare over time.
Study instruction:
- "Build or update recurring-decision checklists during a neutral period, before the next case creates pressure to justify a preferred option."
Key point:
- Advance preparation reduces preference-driven omissions.
- Reusable checklists improve consistency across repeated decisions.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
42. Q: How can a team develop a shared checklist for a recurring decision?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests asking group members independently what they would need to know to advise on that category of decision. The responses can then be compiled, anonymized for distribution, and discussed as a team. This process allows members to contribute different information needs without being anchored by the first speaker or the most senior person's preferred list. The team can combine the suggestions into a master checklist and agree that it will be used whenever advice is requested on that type of decision. Periodic review can keep the checklist relevant as goals, resources, policies, or operating conditions change.
Study instruction:
- "Ask each team member privately to list required facts, compile the responses anonymously, agree a master checklist, and assign an owner for periodic review."
Key point:
- A shared checklist can be built from independently elicited information needs.
- Team agreement makes the checklist a common standard for requesting and providing advice.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
43. Q: What accountability does a requester have when asking others for feedback?
A: Show answer
The requester is responsible for supplying the relevant information on the agreed checklist. The requester should not assume that the adviser will infer omitted facts or recognize that an important detail is missing. The chapter notes that people can interpret emphasized facts as especially important and omitted facts as unimportant. A complete, balanced brief reduces the chance that the requester's narrative shapes the answer. The requester should be willing to answer clarifying questions and acknowledge when required information is unavailable, rather than asking for a confident conclusion from an inadequate information set.
Study instruction:
- "Before sending a request, confirm each checklist item is answered or explicitly marked as unavailable, unknown, or not applicable."
Key point:
- The seeker of feedback must provide the information needed for a sound assessment.
- Explicitly identify gaps rather than allowing omissions to mislead the adviser.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
44. Q: What accountability does a person giving feedback have when key information is missing?
A: Show answer
The person giving feedback should ask for missing information and refuse to offer a substantive opinion when the information required for a meaningful assessment is unavailable. The chapter presents refusal not as rudeness but as a form of kindness: giving an unsupported answer can mislead the requester and reinforce poor decision habits. Advisers often feel pressure to be helpful or may overestimate the value of their own opinions. A checklist and an agreed expectation make it legitimate to pause rather than improvise. The adviser can explain which facts are missing and what would be needed before a responsible recommendation can be made.
Study instruction:
- "Practise responding to incomplete requests by naming the missing facts and explaining why they are necessary before giving advice."
Key point:
- An adviser should not present an uninformed guess as useful feedback.
- Requesting missing facts or declining to advise protects decision quality.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
45. Q: What does the poker example teach about refusing to give feedback without a key fact?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes students asking whether they should have called a bet on the final card while being unable to remember the pot size. The author refused to answer because the pot size was essential to evaluating the choice; without it, the advice would have no value and could be misleading. The refusal also taught the students to pay attention to the pot in future hands and to understand why the information mattered. The lesson extends beyond poker: a person seeking advice must supply the facts that determine the decision, and an adviser should not fill critical gaps with confident speculation.
Study instruction:
- "For a decision that depends on a key quantity, identify the quantity explicitly and do not provide a verdict when it is unavailable."
Key point:
- Missing a critical variable can make advice meaningless.
- Refusal can reinforce the habit of collecting essential information next time.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
46. Q: How can a checklist help prevent narrative bias in future decisions?
A: Show answer
A checklist provides a pre-agreed framework for the information that must be considered and shared. It reduces the opportunity for a requester to highlight only details that support a preferred conclusion or omit inconvenient facts. It can also train people to notice information they previously neglected, as in the poker example where students learned to track the pot size. Over repeated decisions, the checklist encourages more complete observation and more consistent feedback. It does not guarantee that every item is interpreted correctly, so the checklist should be reviewed and supplemented when unusual circumstances arise.
Study instruction:
- "Use the checklist on several real cases and note whether it captures the facts that later prove important; revise it when recurring gaps appear."
Key point:
- A checklist makes critical information visible before advice is given.
- A checklist improves consistency but should remain open to relevant exceptions.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
47. Q: What does the chapter mean by treating decisions as a portfolio?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares a person's decisions over a lifetime with an investment portfolio. The objective is not to make every individual decision turn out well, because luck and incomplete information mean that some outcomes will be poor even when the choice was reasonable. The objective is for the collection of decisions, taken together, to move the person toward their goals and values. The house-flipping analogy illustrates that an investor may lose money on one property while the overall portfolio can still be successful. Likewise, evaluating one choice only by its result can obscure the quality of the process across many decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Review a series of decisions over time and assess whether the overall process advances your goals, rather than judging only the most recent outcome."
Key point:
- Good decision-making is a portfolio-level practice, not a promise of perfect individual outcomes.
- A single win or loss does not settle whether the overall decision process is good.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
48. Q: Why can a good decision lead to a bad outcome?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that outcomes are affected by luck and incomplete information. A decision-maker may identify sensible options, evaluate the available evidence, and choose the option with the best expected path, yet events outside their control may produce an unfavourable result. If every bad result is treated as proof of bad judgment, people may learn the wrong lesson and become overly defensive. The same issue applies in reverse: a poor process can occasionally produce a good result by luck. Decision quality must therefore be assessed using the information and reasoning available at the time, with the outcome considered as evidence but not as the only criterion.
Study instruction:
- "For a disappointing outcome, list what was knowable at the time, what was outside your control, and which parts of the process were sound or weak."
Key point:
- Luck and incomplete information can separate decision quality from outcome quality.
- Do not infer process quality from the result alone.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
49. Q: How can a person learn from a good outcome without over-crediting the decision?
A: Show answer
A favourable result can encourage someone to assume that the decision process was excellent, even if the result depended heavily on luck or circumstances that were not predictable. The chapter's final checklist asks whether there were ways the decision could have been better even though the outcome was good. It also asks what other outcomes could reasonably have occurred and which factors lay outside the decision-maker's control. This review prevents success from becoming automatic evidence that every assumption or action was sound. It supports learning by identifying what to retain and what to improve before the next decision.
Study instruction:
- "After a success, review the assumptions, evidence, alternatives, and controllable steps as critically as you would after a failure."
Key point:
- A good result does not prove that the process could not be improved.
- Use success reviews to identify strengths and remaining process weaknesses.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
50. Q: What is the purpose of the chapter's resulting checklist?
A: Show answer
The resulting checklist is designed to separate decision quality from the influence of the outcome. It asks how much the result is clouding the review, whether good decisions occurred along the way despite a bad result, whether the process could have been better despite a good result, which factors were outside the decision-maker's control, and what other outcomes could have occurred. These questions prevent a reviewer from reducing a complex decision to a simple success-or-failure label. They encourage examination of both the decision process and the role of luck, including alternative paths that were plausible at the time.
Study instruction:
- "Use each resulting-checklist question in a post-decision review before assigning a simple good or bad label."
Key point:
- The checklist asks reviewers to examine process, luck, and alternative outcomes.
- The result should not dominate the evaluation of the decision.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
51. Q: What is hindsight bias, and which phrases can signal it?
A: Show answer
Hindsight bias is the tendency to see an outcome as more predictable after it has occurred than it really was before it happened. The chapter's checklist identifies phrases such as 'I should have known it,' 'I told you so,' and 'I knew it all along' as cues that a person may be reconstructing the past around present knowledge. These statements can make an uncertain result seem inevitable and can lead people to underestimate how difficult the original choice was. Recognizing the language is a starting point; the next step is to compare what was actually known at the time with information revealed later.
Study instruction:
- "Notice hindsight phrases during reviews and ask what information was genuinely available before the outcome became known."
Key point:
- Hindsight makes past outcomes appear more predictable than they were.
- Language such as 'I knew it all along' is a cue to examine the evidence record.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
52. Q: How does the chapter recommend addressing hindsight bias in a past decision review?
A: Show answer
The appendix checklist recommends asking whether any information was revealed only after the fact, whether that information was reasonably knowable when the decision was made, and whether the claim that the outcome was predictable depends on information that was unavailable at the time. After answering these questions, the reviewer reassesses how predictable the outcome actually was. A contemporaneous journal or decision record can help establish the information set that existed then. The process does not excuse poor preparation; it helps distinguish preventable oversights from facts that genuinely could not have been known.
Study instruction:
- "Use the four-step hindsight checklist and consult time-stamped records before revising your estimate of how predictable the result was."
Key point:
- Separate later-revealed information from information reasonably knowable at the time.
- Reassess predictability only after reconstructing the original information set.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
53. Q: What is the Decision Multiverse checklist?
A: Show answer
The Decision Multiverse checklist asks the reviewer to identify the decision, identify the actual outcome, create a simple decision tree containing other reasonable outcomes that were possible at the time, and explore those alternatives to understand what can be learned from the result that occurred. The purpose is to prevent the actual path from becoming the only path considered in hindsight. Thinking through plausible alternatives helps clarify how luck, uncertainty, and the choice itself contributed to the result. It also encourages a more balanced account of what the decision-maker could reasonably have expected.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a small decision tree showing the actual result and other realistic outcomes that could have followed from the same decision."
Key point:
- The actual outcome is only one branch among the reasonable possibilities.
- Exploring alternative branches improves learning from a realised result.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
54. Q: How does the chapter's Three Ps checklist structure a decision evaluation?
A: Show answer
The appendix presents six steps: identify the reasonable set of possible outcomes; identify how much each outcome is liked or disliked in light of the decision-maker's values and payoffs; estimate the likelihood of each outcome; assess the relative likelihood of desirable and undesirable outcomes for the option; repeat the analysis for the other options; and compare the options. Gains represent upside and losses represent downside. The checklist combines possible outcomes, preferences or payoffs, and probabilities rather than relying on a simple list of pros and cons. It gives a repeatable way to compare alternatives while making uncertainty explicit.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the six steps to a real choice and make sure each option is assessed using the same outcome, preference, and likelihood logic."
Key point:
- Evaluate possible outcomes, preferences/payoffs, and likelihoods for each option.
- Compare options after applying the same structured analysis to each.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
55. Q: How does the 'Taking Dead Aim' checklist improve probability estimates?
A: Show answer
The checklist asks the decision-maker to use a survey of how people interpret common probability terms, translate an initial verbal estimate into a more specific bull's-eye estimate, and set a reasonable upper and lower bound. It recommends testing those bounds with a shock test and asking what new information could change the estimate. If that information is available, the decision-maker should seek it; if it is not, they should watch for it later. The method makes vague confidence more precise and creates a mechanism for updating an estimate when relevant evidence appears.
Study instruction:
- "Convert vague probability language into a numeric estimate and range, test the bounds, and write down the evidence that would change the estimate."
Key point:
- Specific estimates and ranges make uncertainty easier to inspect.
- A good forecast identifies what evidence would cause an update.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
56. Q: How does the 'Turning Decisions Outside In' checklist combine outside and inside views?
A: Show answer
The checklist asks the decision-maker to describe the situation entirely from the outside view, including applicable base rates and perspectives from other people; describe it from the inside view; and then find the intersection of the two to develop a more accurate narrative. The outside view helps counter a story that is built only from the specifics and preferences of the current case. The inside view preserves the case-specific facts that make this decision distinctive. Combining them avoids treating general experience as a substitute for individual evidence, while also preventing a compelling personal narrative from ignoring relevant base rates and outside perspectives.
Study instruction:
- "Write separate outside-view and inside-view descriptions before combining them into a balanced decision narrative."
Key point:
- The outside view contributes base rates and other perspectives.
- The inside view contributes case-specific detail; the strongest narrative uses both.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
57. Q: How can a decision-maker use the chapter's checklists in a complete feedback process?
A: Show answer
A practical process begins by identifying the decision and the information needed to assess it. The requester creates or consults a checklist of goals, values, resources, and material facts; removes their preferred answer and outcome from the initial brief; frames the request neutrally; and specifies the form of feedback required. Individual respondents submit initial views independently, with anonymity used where status could contaminate responses. The group then reviews both shared and divergent information, asks clarifying questions, and considers the relevant expertise once the content has been examined. For a past decision, feedback is staged to preserve the original information set. The process ends with a documented review of assumptions, alternatives, uncertainty, and lessons.
Study instruction:
- "Build a one-page procedure that combines neutral framing, complete facts, independent responses, appropriate anonymity, and outcome-aware review."
Key point:
- Decision hygiene is a system of linked safeguards, not one isolated technique.
- The method should protect both the independence and the informational quality of feedback.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
58. Q: What additional safeguards does the chapter recommend for a group decision-hygiene checklist?
A: Show answer
The chapter's group checklist adds two explicit practices to the individual feedback safeguards: solicit feedback independently before group discussion or before members express views to one another, and anonymize the sources of those initial views while distributing a compilation for review in advance of the meeting. These measures address the risk that the first public voice will anchor everyone else and that high-status contributors will receive disproportionate influence. The group should still specify the form of the output and use a shared information checklist so that members assess the decision on an adequate and comparable basis.
Study instruction:
- "Before the next group decision, schedule an independent submission stage and a first-pass anonymous review as standing agenda steps."
Key point:
- Independent collection and initial anonymity are core group safeguards.
- Use these steps alongside a complete information brief and defined feedback format.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
59. Q: What would a hiring committee do to reduce belief and outcome contamination?
A: Show answer
A hiring committee can circulate the same job requirements, resources, and candidate evidence to each member, then ask members to submit their preferred candidate and rationale privately before any group discussion. The committee should avoid revealing a senior member's preference or the eventual hiring outcome before initial views are recorded. If members need to assess the decision at multiple stages, the committee can iterate feedback: first discuss the role and salary range, then the process, then the available candidate evidence, while withholding the requester's chosen action at each stage. An anonymous first-pass compilation can help the committee consider each rationale before identities and status influence the discussion.
Study instruction:
- "For a real hiring review, prepare a common fact pack, independent recommendation form, and staged disclosure plan before the committee meets."
Key point:
- Common information and independent recommendations reduce early anchoring.
- Review each hiring stage using only information available at that stage.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
60. Q: How could an investment committee apply Chapter 9's group decision hygiene?
A: Show answer
An investment committee can ask each member to assess the investment independently before discussion, using a defined format for assumptions, risks, possible outcomes, and rationale. Initial views can be anonymized so that an executive's preference does not dominate the first discussion. Members should identify facts that are unique to their area of expertise and make sure those facts are surfaced rather than left in individual files. The group should distinguish an expected return from a realised result and avoid using later performance as the only measure of the original decision. For repeated investment decisions, a prebuilt checklist can specify the financial, strategic, and operational information every reviewer must receive.
Study instruction:
- "Create an investment-review template that captures independent assessments, unique information, decision-time assumptions, and relevant downside scenarios."
Key point:
- Independent and structured views help expose divergent assumptions and risks.
- Evaluate the investment decision using the information available at the time, not performance alone.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
61. Q: What should a decision-maker do when a respondent asks to hear their opinion before answering?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that respondents sometimes ask for the requester's view because they want to avoid unintentionally disagreeing. The requester should explain that they are deliberately collecting an independent first opinion and invite the respondent to answer before hearing the requester's preference. The requester can reassure the person that divergence is useful and will not be treated as disrespect. If the respondent still needs clarification, the requester should provide factual context without revealing the conclusion. After the initial view and rationale are recorded, the requester may disclose their own position and discuss the difference.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a neutral explanation such as 'I want your first, independent view, so I will share my own after you have answered.'"
Key point:
- The requester can protect independence by delaying disclosure of their view.
- Make it clear that a divergent answer is welcome and useful.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
62. Q: How can a facilitator respond when a consensus begins forming too quickly?
A: Show answer
The facilitator should pause the discussion and explicitly invite members to identify facts or interpretations that do not support the emerging preference. If initial views have not yet been collected, the facilitator can ask each member to write down an independent position and rationale before continuing. The facilitator should ask who has information that others may not possess and request that it be shared before a decision is finalized. A structured round can include junior members first or use anonymous submissions. The goal is not to manufacture disagreement but to make sure apparent consensus has not formed merely because dissenting information was never heard.
Study instruction:
- "When consensus appears early, pause and request contrary evidence, unique facts, and independent written views before the group votes."
Key point:
- Early agreement can be a sign that unshared information has not been surfaced.
- A facilitator should test the basis of consensus before treating it as informed agreement.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
- Stasser and Titus, Pooling of Unshared Information in Group Decision Making (1985)
63. Q: What are the limits of decision hygiene as described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Decision hygiene improves the process through which information and feedback are gathered, but it does not eliminate all bias, ensure that every respondent is correct, or guarantee a good outcome. A neutral question can still be answered from incomplete knowledge. Anonymous views can still contain errors. Independent feedback can still be distorted if the requester provides a slanted or partial brief. Expert advice can still be biased, and a careful decision can still lead to an unfavourable result because of luck or incomplete information. The chapter's checklists therefore function as safeguards and learning tools, not as mechanical guarantees. They should be adapted to the importance, reversibility, and information needs of each decision.
Study instruction:
- "For an important decision, list which safeguards were used and which risks or uncertainties remain despite using them."
Key point:
- Decision hygiene reduces avoidable contamination but does not remove uncertainty.
- A good process must still be evaluated, updated, and adapted to the decision.
Source links:
- Annie Duke, How to Decide — publisher book page
